## A R T I C L E I N F O                                      A B S T R A C T

Keywords:                                                  The water requirements of plants are crucial for the sustainable water resources management since in the Actual evapotranspiration                                  Mediterranean region, agriculture is the major consumer of water. In recent decades, remote sensing (Landsat, Remote sensing                                             Sentinel, etc) has been used with appropriate methodologies that allow the estimation of evapotranspiration. The Sentinel proposed methodology in this research has been developed in parallel with a similar methodology by the Eu­ Experiments ropean Space Agency (ESA), based on a double energy balance. The proposed method has received an Maize Cotton                                                     improvement as compared to the ESA’s method, since the Weather Research & Forecasting Model (WRF) is used Hay                                                        instead of ERA$_5$, the fifth generation ECMWF, for better estimation of the meteorological conditions. Despite the improvement, the method still presents a systematic overestimation in relation to the field values mainly in the second half of the growing season. To address this problem and to successfully estimate daily actual evapo­ transpiration (ETa) values, the calibration and validation of the method for two crops (maize, cotton) and hay (mixture of forage crops) is presented. The first two crops were based on experiments carried out in central Greece and the third (hay) in experimental fields in southern France. Soil-water balance models, AquaCrop (for Greece) and PILOTE (for France), are used to monitor the daily crop irrigation water requirements throughout the growing season. The adopted satellite ETa adjustment factor in all crops is close to 0.59–0.63 and R$_2$ ranges from 0.87 to 0.97. The results show that a reliable estimation of ETa can be obtained using satellite data. [SOURCE: Page_2]

Introduction                                                                                      A universal call to action, the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) direct to "protect the planet and ensure that all humanity enjoys peace As global population grows with rapidly increasing demands                                   and prosperity." These goals are all linked and integrated, balancing the (increased food production, urbanization and industrial development),                            social, economic, and environmental aspects of sustainable develop­ agriculture, the world’s largest water consumer representing around                              ment, to guarantee their accomplishment. Many of the 17 SDGs, entitled 70 % of global freshwater use, faces challenges as water resources are                           No Poverty (SDG$_1$), Zero Hunger (SDG$_2$), Gender Equality (SDG$_5$), becoming scarce and under pressure due to climate change/extreme                                 Responsible Consumption and Production (SDG12), Climate Action climate variability, worldwide competition and increased agriculture                             (SDG13), Life Below Water (SDG14), and Life on Land (SDG15), are adaptation. Therefore, there is a critical need for sustainable manage­                          related to agriculture and climate change as well. Precision agriculture, ment practices in agriculture (Aguirre-García et al., 2021; Kourgialas,                          which aims to assure resilient and sustainable food production and 2021; Kisekka et al., 2022).                                                                     climate adaptation, is required to meet the objectives while addressing [SOURCE: Page_2]

The Weather Research and Forecasting (WRF) Model is a regional atmospheric modeling system designed for both research and arith­ metical weather prediction. WRF is an open-source model, and it has 2.1. State-of-the-art been tested and applied for research at universities and at governmental laboratories, for operational forecasting by state and private organiza­ In this research, combined Sentinel-2 and Sentinel-3 images are used tions, and for commercial applications by industry. WRF was developed to calculate daily ETa,satactual crop evapotranspiration. The simulation et al., 2019). program utilized is the Sen-ET SNAP software (ESA, 2020). ### 2.6. The PILOTE crop model 15.52 % 90

The PILOTE model was chosen for the application case of France, since this model was already calibrated on the study area. The Crau hay 14.98 % [SOURCE: Page_8]

version (Mailhol and Merot, 2008) of the PILOTE crop model has been utilized and adapted for this study to fit with the new auto PILOTE 60 [SOURCE: Page_8]

procedure (Cheviron et al., 2016), which enables the generation and analysis of multiple scenarios in procedures that are intended for con­ 15.10 % Farmer [SOURCE: Page_8]

strained optimization. The model requires four climatic forcing (rain and/or irrigation, temperature, potential evapotranspiration, and total 30 [SOURCE: Page_8]

incoming radiation) at a daily timestep, which is also the requirement for the predictions it delivers; water reserve and drainage, soil evapo­ 14.87 % [SOURCE: Page_8]

ration, plant transpiration, LAI and total dry matter (TDM) are some of the daily updated calculated variables. PILOTE is a true crop model 15 [SOURCE: Page_8]

because a reciprocal control loop exists between the evolution of the water reserve in soils and crop growth. The status of the water reserve (i. 9.35 % Soil moisture measurements at the soil moisture sensors’ depth. [SOURCE: Page_8]

e., filling of the superficial, root zone and deep reservoirs) is updated 90 [SOURCE: Page_8]

from rain and/or irrigation events using the values of a series of soil parameters (e.g., field capacity and depths of the previously mentioned reservoirs). The dynamics of plant growth are controlled by the accu­ 11.99 % [SOURCE: Page_8]

mulation of degrees-days (a sum of mean daily air temperature), the 60 [SOURCE: Page_8]

values of several shape parameters (that dictate the shape of the LAI curve with increasing degrees-days) and the occurrence of water stresses 13.28 % [SOURCE: Page_8]

(especially harmful for this crop). Root development is calculated in HubIS [SOURCE: Page_8]

coherence with the increase of LAI, which allows the estimation of soil 30 [SOURCE: Page_8]

evaporation and plant transpiration from the climatic demand (potential evapotranspiration). This closes PILOTE’s calculation loop: root water 13.11 % [SOURCE: Page_8]

uptake and water losses by evaporation (or drainage) provide the last Maize [SOURCE: Page_8]

update to the water reserve terms, at the end of the calculation step, 15 [SOURCE: Page_8]

within a single day. The production of TDM is calculated from the LAI values and the incoming radiation, with the key parameter being the Soil Moisture Type of field Type of crop [SOURCE: Page_8]

radiation use efficiency, while the water stresses have a negative impact Depth (cm) [SOURCE: Page_8]

on the process. An additional feature of the latest (unpublished yet) Table 2 [SOURCE: Page_8]

version of the model is the possibility to “overrule” predicted leaf area index values by independent leaf area values, obtained from field [SOURCE: Page_8]

7 [SOURCE: Page_8]

2021; Sofiati et al., 2025), especially on spring (Varga and Breuer, 2024a), 10 m wind speed (Iriza et al., 2016; Pappa et al., 2023), wind speed under different planetary boundary layer (PBL) physics schemes (Yu et al., 2022), amplitude of soil moisture (Pappa et al., 2023), and solar radiation under a mean diurnal cycle, especially after midday (Pappa et al., 2023), at various spatial resolutions over several regions, resulted in their overestimation. In other studies, the WRF output overestimates the lower wind speeds, while underestimates the strong wind speeds (Giannaros et al., 2017; Solbakken and Birkelund, 2018). Solar radiation is a major weather parameter with increased difficulty in being downscaled from global to regional scale, having remarkable overestimation against observed data (Srivastava et al., 2015). Yet, the minimum temperature WRF underestimation was pointed by Varga and Breuer (2024b) in low-altitude regions in the presence of snow cover. In Fig. 13 and Fig. 14, the ETa (maize and cotton) calculated by the AquaCrop is represented by a continuous line, while the corresponding one from the satellite images is illustrated with points (growing season 2021), the latter being overestimated in comparison with the agronomic simulation. For this reason, a regression between AquaCrop and Sentinel ETa data was carried out and an adjustment factor was calculated to improve the accuracy of the satellite method. Y WUE =                                                                         (6) ETa More recent studies denote that the ratio of the physical crop yield, or the marketable yield, over actual evapotranspiration, is referred to crop water productivity (CWP) (Blatchford et al., 2018; Fernández et al., 2020; Tarkalson et al., 2022; Mehmood et al., 2023). For the 2022 growing season, regarding the maize field, irrigation planning led to WUE equal to 3.11 kg/m^3 in the HubIS plot, whereas equal to 3.71 kg/m^3 in the Farmer plot. As for the cotton field, the crop irrigation planning slightly improved the WUE (0.94 kg/m^3 in the HubIS plot instead of 0.93 kg/m^3 in the Farmer plot). Regarding 2023, the simulated yields for both crops were increased enough in relation to the actual yields. The reduce of the observed yields of the studied fields is justified from the significant damages caused to the farms from the passage of storm Daniel over the Thessaly plain on September 4–7, 2023, leading to flooding. The daily actual evapotranspiration with satellite ETa (sat) values for maize and cotton regarding 2022 and 2023, considering the respective adjustment factors (validation periods), are presented (HubIS and Fig. 16. N. Dercas et al.                                                                                                        Agricultural Water Management 319 (2025) 109719 [SOURCE: Page_20]

Fig. 24. ETa PILOTE (continuous line) and ETa, sat from satellite observation (points) for the Hay Experimental Fields, in 2021. [SOURCE: Page_20]

using satellite data. The ESA-developed method based on the dual en­ ergy balance was improved in two ways: 1) it used the reliable WRF model in place of ERA$_5$, the fifth generation of ECMWF to estimate the meteorological parameters, and 2) it was calibrated and validated against field measurements and simulations in experimental fields in Greece (for maize and cotton), and in France (for hay). The results of satellite estimates and field estimates (through measurements and sim­ ulations) showed differences up to 50 %, while the improvement had already been made using WRF. By determining a correction/adjustment factor, which in all cases was ~0.60 (R$_2$=0.87–0.97), a good estimate of the daily evapotranspiration for the three crops was achieved. Finally, this approach is particularly useful since it allows reliable estimates of actual crop water consumption, which will permit us to effectively guide farmers in their irrigation, while at the same time, it will help us in the overall management of water resources in wider areas. However, the results for the method validation carried out lead us to conclude that the estimation of the actual daily evapotranspiration is likely to deviate significantly from the actual one in the field. Yet, for the calculation of a longer period, for example at a weekly step or more, the estimation of ETa will be improved. In the context of climate change adaptation, future agronomic simulation scenarios might concern the application of the same irriga­ Fig. 25. Regression between PILOTE and Sentinel values for Hay, in 2021. tion rates in the monitored fields of annual spring crops which are cultivated in Thessaly (maize, cotton) under the climate scenarios of [SOURCE: Page_20]
